=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given permutations P = (P_1, P_2, \ldots, P_N) and A = (A_1, A_2, \ldots, A_N) of (1,2,\ldots,N).
You can perform the following operation any number of times, possibly zero:

- replace A_i with A_{P_i} simultaneously for all i=1,2,\ldots,N.

Print the lexicographically smallest A that can be obtained.
What is lexicographical order?
 For sequences of length N, A = (A_1, A_2, \ldots, A_N) and B = (B_1, B_2, \ldots, B_N), A is lexicographically smaller than B if and only if:

- there exists an integer i\ (1\leq i\leq N) such that A_i < B_i, and A_j = B_j for all 1\leq j < i.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
P_1 P_2 \ldots P_N
A_1 A_2 \ldots A_N

Output

Let (A_1, A_2, \ldots, A_N) be the lexicographically smallest A that can be obtained. Print A_1, A_2, \ldots, A_N in this order, separated by spaces, in one line.

Constraints


- 1\leq N\leq2\times10^5
- 1\leq P_i\leq N\ (1\leq i\leq N)
- P_i\neq P_j\ (1\leq i<j\leq N)
- 1\leq A_i\leq N\ (1\leq i\leq N)
- A_i\neq A_j\ (1\leq i<j\leq N)
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

6
3 1 5 6 2 4
4 3 1 6 2 5

Sample Output 1

1 4 2 5 3 6

Initially, A = (4, 3, 1, 6, 2, 5).
Repeating the operation yields the following.

- A = (1, 4, 2, 5, 3, 6)
- A = (2, 1, 3, 6, 4, 5)
- A = (3, 2, 4, 5, 1, 6)
- A = (4, 3, 1, 6, 2, 5)

After this, A will revert to the original state every four operations.
Therefore, print the lexicographically smallest among these, which is 1 4 2 5 3 6.

Sample Input 2

8
3 5 8 7 2 6 1 4
1 2 3 4 5 6 7 8

Sample Output 2

1 2 3 4 5 6 7 8

You may choose to perform no operations.

Sample Input 3

26
24 14 4 20 15 19 16 11 23 22 12 18 21 3 6 8 26 2 25 7 13 1 5 9 17 10
15 3 10 1 13 19 22 24 20 4 14 23 7 26 25 18 11 6 9 12 2 21 5 16 8 17

Sample Output 3

4 1 22 18 20 13 14 6 15 11 3 26 2 12 5 23 9 10 25 24 7 17 16 21 19 8

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Data structures:
- P list 1-indexed or 0-indexed.

- For each cycle, we need maybe arrays values in cycle order: val_at_offset[t] = A[cycle[t]].

When processing index i:
If cycle of i already has a fixed residue k_mod_m? Actually once we process a cycle, we impose a congruence k ≡ res_c mod m_c. We can store for each cycle c a residue res[c] (0 <= res[c] < m_c). Initially -1.
If res[cycle_id] != -1, then for this index i, value is determined: A[cycle[(off[i]+res_c)%m]]. No decision. But wait if current candidate set has been refined after this cycle was fixed, res_c remains the same; yes k mod m fixed. So output value = A[cycle[(off[i]+res_c)%m]].
If res[cycle_id] == -1 (first time encountering this cycle in natural index order), we need choose res_c. The current candidate set before adding this cycle is k ≡ r mod M (where M lcm of processed cycles). We need find s = res_c modulo m such that:
  1. k ≡ r mod M and k ≡ s mod m are compatible, i.e., s ≡ r mod g where g = gcd(M,m).
  2. Among all s satisfying this, value A[cycle[(off[i]+s)%m]] is minimal? Wait P^k(i) = cycle[(off[i]+k)%m]. Let k ≡ s mod m. Then offset = (off[i]+s) mod m. Condition s ≡ r mod g. We need choose s in that residue class modulo m (i.e., s = r + tM mod m) minimizing A[cycle[(off[i]+s)%m]]. Since as s varies over residue class modulo g, the position offset (off[i]+s) mod m also varies over residue class modulo g. Equivalently positions p = cycle[t] with t ≡ off[i]+r mod g. Need find t in [0,m-1] with t ≡ (off[i]+r) mod g that minimizes A[p]. Then s = (t - off[i]) mod m. Note s will be congruent to r mod g automatically.
So we need find minimal A among positions in the cycle whose offset t is congruent to target_mod_g = (off[i] + r_mod_g) mod g, where g = gcd(M,m). Here r_mod_g = r mod g. Need compute r_mod_g.

Then if multiple positions have same A? A values are distinct, so unique minimal value. Thus unique t. Then res_c = (t - off[i]) mod m. This is the chosen residue modulo m. Update global CRT state with new congruence k ≡ res_c mod m.

Let's design data structures in detail.

Input N up to 2e5. Use 0-index.

P: list length N, convert to 0-index: P[i] = p_i - 1.
A: list length N, values maybe 1-index; store as int.

Cycle decomposition:
visited = [False]*N
cycles = []  # list of lists of indices in order following P
cycle_id = [-1]*N
off = [0]*N
cycle_len = []
For i in range(N):
 if not visited[i]:
   cur = i
   cyc = []
   while not visited[cur]:
      visited[cur]=True
      cycle_id[cur]=len(cycles)
      off[cur]=len(cyc)
      cyc.append(cur)
      cur = P[cur]
   # Because P is permutation, starting unvisited will return to i, not into existing cycle? Since permutation, if start unvisited, following P cannot enter previously visited cycle because indegree 1; it must close at i. So okay.
   cycles.append(cyc)
   cycle_len.append(len(cyc))
But note if we mark visited as we go, when cur returns to i, visited true and loop stops. Good.

For each cycle, vals = [A[idx] for idx in cyc] maybe store in cycles_vals list parallel. Could also compute on fly. Need scan cycle to find min. We can store cyc and vals.

M state:
M_exp = {}  # prime -> exponent in M
rem = {}    # prime -> current residue modulo p^M_exp[p]
We need factor numbers up to N. Precompute smallest prime factor (spf) up to N. Then factor m quickly.

Function factorize(x):
   factors = {} # prime -> exponent
   while x > 1:
      p = spf[x]
      cnt=0
      while x%p==0: x//=p; cnt++
      factors[p]=cnt
   return factors
Need for m up to N. We can precompute factorization for all numbers? N 2e5, factorizing per cycle with spf O(total exponents) ~ N loglog? Fine. But each cycle once; total factorization cost O(sum omega * log) small. Could also precompute prime factors list for each m? Not necessary.

Need compute g and r_mod_g for current cycle length m:
factors_m = factorize(m)
g = 1
prime_powers = []  # list (p, e_g, pk)
for p, e_m in factors_m.items():
    E = M_exp.get(p, 0)
    e_g = min(E, e_m)
    if e_g > 0:
        pk = p ** e_g
        g *= pk
        # residue modulo pk from rem[p]
        a = rem[p] % pk  # rem[p] is modulo p^E, E>=e_g
        prime_powers.append((p, e_g, pk, a))
    # if E=0, e_g=0, p not in g
Then r_mod_g = CRT of prime_powers. If g==1, r_mod_g=0.
Need compute CRT:
def crt_list(congruences):
    x=0; mod=1
    for p,e,pk,a in congruences:
        # combine x mod mod with a mod pk
        if mod == 1:
            x = a % pk
            mod = pk
        else:
            # mod and pk coprime
            t = ((a - x) % pk) * pow(mod % pk, -1, pk) % pk
            x = x + mod * t
            mod *= pk
    return x % g? Actually x is modulo mod = g. Since congruences cover all prime powers in g, mod = g. Return x.
Need pow inverse available Python 3.8+. AtCoder Python supports. Could implement extended gcd for safety? pow(a,-1,mod) is okay in PyPy 3.10? AtCoder uses PyPy 3.10 yes. But if mod=1 skip. Since pk maybe 1? no.

Now, complexity:
- Cycle decomposition O(N).
- For each cycle, factor m O(log m) with spf.
- For each cycle, compute g and r_mod_g: factor m, iterate distinct primes <= ~6, compute pow(p,e_g), CRT with pow inverse. O(log N) per cycle.
- Scan cycle to find min: total O(N).
- Output O(N).
Memory O(N).

This seems good.

But wait: We scan each cycle to find min at first encounter. However, the target residue class depends on r_mod_g, which depends on current M. M is lcm of previously processed cycles (those whose first index appeared earlier in natural order). This is not necessarily all cycles processed before in scan order? We process indices natural order. If a cycle's first index appears later, its length not in M yet. Good. The scan of a cycle happens when first index appears. At that time, M includes only cycles whose first index appeared earlier. Is that the correct candidate set after previous coordinates? Yes, because previous indices may include multiple indices from same cycles; but constraints only from cycles whose first occurrence appeared. If a cycle's first occurrence hasn't appeared, no constraint from it yet. But could a previous index belong to a cycle whose first occurrence was later? Impossible: first occurrence is the earliest index in natural order; if a previous index belongs to that cycle, then first occurrence would be that previous index or earlier. So M exactly cycles with first occurrence < i. Good.

Now, let's test with a small brute force to validate algorithm conceptually. We can't run, but can manually enumerate random small.

Example N=3. P cycles: (1 2) length2, (3) length1. A = [2,1,3]? Let's 0-index P: 0->1,1->0,2->2. A=[2,1,3].
Operation k=0: [2,1,3]; k=1: A[P(i)]: i0 A1=1, i1 A0=2, i2 A2=3 => [1,2,3]. Lex min [1,2,3] k=1.
Algorithm:

Now, what if a cycle length m has gcd(M,m)=g >1 and the minimal reachable value occurs at t. We set res_c = t - off_i mod m. Potential pitfalls:
- The lcm M can be huge but we avoid. However, our rem per prime exponent update assumes that the current candidate set after processing some cycles is exactly a single residue modulo M. Is it always a single residue? We choose a unique residue for each processed cycle. The intersection of congruences modulo cycle lengths is a single residue modulo lcm if compatible. Yes. But what if two processed cycles have lengths not coprime and the chosen residues are compatible? They must be because they came from actual k in previous S. We maintain compatibility. Good.
- When we update rem for a prime p with e_m > old_E by setting to res_c % p^{e_m}, we ignore the old residue modulo p^{old_E}. But compatibility ensures it matches. Need maybe if old_E=0, no old. Good.
- When e_m <= old_E, we don't update rem. But what if old_E > e_m and the new congruence is not compatible? It should be. But due to algorithm, res_c is chosen compatible with r mod g where g includes p^{e_m} (since e_g = e_m). So res_c ≡ r mod p^{e_m}. rem[p] % p^{e_m} = r mod p^{e_m}. Good.
- Need compute r_mod_g before updating. r_mod_g = current r modulo g. We compute from rem. But if g includes p^{e_g} where e_g = min(E_p, e_m). If E_p=0, e_g=0. Good.

x=0 mod1; p2 pk2 a=1 -> x=1 mod2; p3 pk3 a=2, mod=2, pk=3, t=((2-1)%3)*inv(2 mod3=2)%3 =1*2=2; x=1+2*2=5 mod6. Good.

Now, factorization and prime powers:
Precompute spf:
spf = list(range(N+1))? Standard:

for i in range(2,N+1):
 if spf[i]==0:
   spf[i]=i
   if i*i<=N:
     for j in range(i*i,N+1,i):
        if spf[j]==0: spf[j]=i
But this O(N log log). Simpler:
spf = list(range(N+1))
for i in range(2, int(N**0.5)+1):
 if spf[i]==i:
   for j in range(i*i,N+1,i):
      if spf[j]==j: spf[j]=i
Need spf[1]=1. For N=1, range okay.
factorize(x):
   res={}
   while x>1:
     p=spf[x]
     cnt=0
     while x%p==0:
       x//=p; cnt+=1
     res[p]=cnt
   return res
Since spf[x] set for all x? Need initialize spf = list(range(N+1)); spf[1]=1. For prime i, spf[i]=i. For composite, smallest prime set. Good.

Now, let's consider if the minimal value in a cycle's reachable class can be found by scanning vals with `t % g == target`. Since target = (off_i + r_mod_g) % g. Let's derive:
We need k ≡ r mod M. Let k = r + qM. For cycle length m, k mod m = (r + qM) mod m. As q varies, k mod m ranges over residues congruent to r modulo g = gcd(M,m). Specifically k ≡ r mod g. Then position t = (off_i + k) mod m. Since k ≡ r mod g, t ≡ off_i + r mod g. Conversely, for any t with that congruence, does there exist k satisfying k ≡ r mod M and k ≡ t - off_i mod m? Yes, because t - off_i ≡ r mod g, so CRT compatible. Thus reachable positions exactly t ≡ target mod g. Good.

Now, after choosing best_t, res_c = (best_t - off_i) mod m. This is the required k mod m. It satisfies res_c ≡ r mod g. Good.

Now, let's think about the update of rem for primes where e_m > old_E. We set rem[p] = res_c % p^{e_m}. But what if old_E > 0 and e_m > old_E, and res_c % p^{e_m} is not congruent to old rem modulo p^{old_E} due to a bug? We might want to combine instead of overwrite to be safe. Actually if old_E < e_m, the new congruence modulo p^{e_m} is stronger and determines the residue modulo p^{e_m}; the old congruence is implied if compatible. Overwriting is correct. But to be safe, we could compute:
if old_E == 0:
   rem[p] = res_c % pk
else:
   # verify compatibility maybe
   # new_rem = res_c % pk
   # old_mod = p**old_E

No need combine. But wait, what if old_E > 0 and e_m > old_E, but the new congruence is modulo p^{e_m}, which is indeed stronger. However, is it possible that the old congruence modulo p^{old_E} is not implied by the new congruence modulo p^{e_m}? It is implied by reducing new residue modulo p^{old_E}. If compatible, yes. Since new residue is a specific integer mod p^{e_m}; its reduction mod p^{old_E} may or may not equal old rem. But algorithm ensures it does. Good.
If we wanted to be extra robust, we could compute new_rem by CRT between old rem mod p^{old_E} and res_c mod p^{e_m} even when old_E < e_m. But since one modulus divides the other, CRT solution is just res_c mod p^{e_m} if compatible. So same.

    ans[idx] = vals[(t + res_c) % m]
Because for index idx=cycle[t], P^{res_c}(idx)=cycle[(t+res_c)%m], value = A[that] = vals[(t+res_c)%m]. Good.

Now, let's think about if res_c is the chosen k modulo m. We set res_c = (best_t - off_i) mod m. For first index i=cycle[off_i], P^{res_c}(i)=cycle[(off_i+res_c)%m]=cycle[best_t]. Value = vals[best_t]. Good.

Now, let's think about if after setting res_c, for another index j=cycle[t], output value = vals[(t+res_c)%m]. This is the value at P^{res_c}(j). Since k=res_c mod m. Good.

Now, let's consider if final k might be any integer satisfying all res_c; output using res_c per cycle is correct because for each cycle only k mod m matters. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a possibility that two different cycles have same length and our M_exp dict handles lcm; yes.

Now, let's consider if the algorithm's "current r modulo M" after updating with a new cycle length m is correctly represented by rem after setting rem[p] for primes where e_m > old_E. Let's prove by induction:
Initially M=1, no primes, r=0.
Suppose rem[p] = r mod p^{E_p} for all p|M.
Add congruence k ≡ s mod m. New M' = lcm(M,m). For p:
- If e_m <= E_p: E'_p=E_p. The new congruence modulo p^{e_m} is implied by old r modulo p^{E_p} (compatibility). The solution r' modulo p^{E_p} equals r modulo p^{E_p}; rem unchanged.
- If e_m > E_p: E'_p=e_m. The new congruence modulo p^{e_m} is stronger; r' modulo p^{e_m} = s mod p^{e_m} (provided compatible with old). Set rem[p]=s mod p^{e_m}.
For p not dividing m: E'_p=E_p; new congruence no condition modulo p, solution modulo p^{E_p} unchanged. Good.
Thus induction holds.

Need maybe compute compatibility for e_m > E_p: s mod p^{E_p} == r mod p^{E_p}. This is guaranteed by s ≡ r mod g. Does g include p^{E_p} when e_m > E_p? g exponent = min(E_p,e_m)=E_p. Yes. We computed r_mod_g and chose s satisfying s ≡ r mod g, so yes. Good.

Now, let's think about if we need to verify compatibility for e_m <= E_p: s mod p^{e_m} == r mod p^{e_m}. g exponent = e_m. s ≡ r mod g includes p^{e_m}. Yes. Good.

Now, let's consider if the scan chooses best_t based on target = (off_i + r_mod_g) % g. Then res_c = best_t - off_i mod m. Does res_c ≡ r_mod_g mod g? Since best_t ≡ off_i + r_mod_g mod g, subtract off_i gives res_c ≡ r_mod_g mod g. Yes.

Now, let's think about if r_mod_g computed from rem is indeed r mod g. We combine residues rem[p] % p^{e_g}. Since rem[p] = r mod p^{E_p}, reducing gives r mod p^{e_g}. CRT over prime powers gives r mod g. Good.

Let's try to prove invariant more formally:
Let S_i be set of integers k (mod L) such that the first i elements of F(k) are lexicographically minimal possible among all k. Initially S_0 = all.
At step i (0-index), consider coordinate i.

- Else no constraint on k mod m_c yet. Current S is a coset k ≡ R mod M. Coordinate i = A_{P^k(i)} = vals[(off_i + k) mod m_c]. To minimize lexicographically, S_i = {k∈S: k ≡ t*-off_i mod m_c}. This is a new coset modulo lcm(M,m_c). Update state accordingly.
After processing all N indices, S_N is set of k achieving lexicomin vector. Since every cycle has been processed, S_N is a single residue modulo L (or at least all k produce same vector). Output F(k) for any k in S_N. Our stored residues per cycle define such k. Good.

Pseudo-code for main loop:

for i in range(N):
    cid = cycle_id[i]
    if res[cid] != -1:
        continue
    m = cycle_len[cid]
    factors_m = factorize(m)
    # compute g and r_mod_g
    g = 1
    congruences = []
    for p, e_m in factors_m.items():
        E = M_exp.get(p, 0)
        e_g = E if E < e_m else e_m
        if e_g:
            pk = p ** e_g
            g *= pk
            a = rem[p] % pk
            congruences.append((pk, a))
    # CRT
    x = 0
    mod = 1
    for pk, a in congruences:
        if mod == 1:
            x = a % pk
            mod = pk
        else:
            t = ((a - x) % pk) * pow(mod % pk, -1, pk) % pk
            x += mod * t
            mod *= pk
    r_mod_g = x  # mod == g
    off_i = offset[i]
    target = (off_i + r_mod_g) % g
    vals = cycle_vals[cid]
    best_t = -1
    best_val = N + 1
    for t, val in enumerate(vals):
        if t % g == target and val < best_val:
            best_val = val
            best_t = t
    res_c = (best_t - off_i) % m
    res[cid] = res_c
    # update M_exp and rem
    for p, e_m in factors_m.items():
        old_E = M_exp.get(p, 0)
        if e_m > old_E:
            pk = p ** e_m
            rem[p] = res_c % pk
            M_exp[p] = e_m
        # else no change

Wait, need final code only. Let's craft final code carefully.

Need include imports, functions, main guard maybe. Since final answer code block.

Let's write code:

```python
import sys
from math import isqrt

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    N = data[0]
    P = [data[i+1] - 1 for i in range(N)]
    A = [data[1+N+i] for i in range(N)]
    del data

    # smallest prime factor
    spf = list(range(N + 1))
    if N >= 1:
        spf[1] = 1
    for i in range(2, isqrt(N) + 1):
        if spf[i] == i:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            for j in range(start, N + 1, step):
                if spf[j] == j:
                    spf[j] = i

    def factorize(x):
        res = {}
        while x > 1:
            p = spf[x]
            c = 0
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
                c += 1
            res[p] = c
        return res

    visited = [False] * N
    cycles = []
    cycle_id = [-1] * N
    offset = [0] * N
    cycle_len = []

    for i in range(N):
        if not visited[i]:
            cur = i
            cyc = []
            while not visited[cur]:
                visited[cur] = True
                cycle_id[cur] = len(cycles)
                offset[cur] = len(cyc)
                cyc.append(cur)
                cur = P[cur]
            cycles.append(cyc)
            cycle_len.append(len(cyc))

    cycle_vals = []
    for cyc in cycles:
        cycle_vals.append([A[idx] for idx in cyc])
    del A, P, visited

    C = len(cycles)
    res = [-1] * C

    max_exp = [0] * (N + 1)
    rem = [0] * (N + 1)

    for i in range(N):
        cid = cycle_id[i]
        if res[cid] != -1:
            continue

        m = cycle_len[cid]
        fac = factorize(m)

        g = 1
        congruences = []
        for p, e_m in fac.items():
            E = max_exp[p]
            e_g = E if E < e_m else e_m
            if e_g:
                pk = p ** e_g
                g *= pk
                congruences.append((pk, rem[p] % pk))

        x = 0
        mod = 1
        for pk, a in congruences:
            if mod == 1:
                x = a % pk
                mod = pk
            else:
                t = ((a - x) % pk) * pow(mod % pk, -1, pk) % pk
                x += mod * t
                mod *= pk
        r_mod_g = x

        off_i = offset[i]
        target = (off_i + r_mod_g) % g

        vals = cycle_vals[cid]
        best_t = -1
        best_val = N + 1
        for t, val in enumerate(vals):
            if t % g == target and val < best_val:
                best_val = val
                best_t = t

        res_c = (best_t - off_i) % m
        res[cid] = res_c

        for p, e_m in fac.items():
            old_E = max_exp[p]
            if e_m > old_E:
                pk = p ** e_m
                rem[p] = res_c % pk
                max_exp[p] = e_m

    ans = [0] * N
    for c, cyc in enumerate(cycles):
        m = cycle_len[c]
        r = res[c]
        vals = cycle_vals[c]
        for t, idx in enumerate(cyc):
            ans[idx] = vals[(t + r) % m]

    sys.stdout.write(' '.join(map(str, ans)) + '\n')

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Need check if `del A, P, visited` after cycle_vals but before using P? yes. But `P` used only in cycle decomposition. Good.
Potential issue: `spf` for N=1: list(range(2)) = [0,1], spf[1]=1. factorize(1) returns {}. Good.
Potential issue: `max_exp` and `rem` arrays length N+1; if N=1, p never >1. Good.

Let's think about if `del A, P, visited` while `cycle_vals` lists contain ints from A, not references to A list. Good.

Potential issue: `factorize` uses `spf[x]`; for x prime > isqrt(N), spf[x]=x because initialized. Good.

Potential issue: If N=1, `isqrt(N)=1`, range(2,2) empty. Good.

Potential issue: In cycle decomposition, if P is a permutation, while stops at i. But if N=1, visited[0] false, cur=0, while not visited[0]: mark, cycle_id[0]=0, offset[0]=0, append0, cur=P[0]=0, loop stops. cycles [[0]]. Good.

Potential issue: `del data` after P/A; okay.

Potential issue: `best_t` if no t? no.

Potential issue: If `congruences` empty, x=0, r_mod_g=0, g=1. Good.

Potential issue: `pow(mod % pk, -1, pk)` if mod%pk=0? no. Good.

Potential issue: In CRT, `mod` after combining should equal g. But if congruences list empty, mod=1, g=1. Good.

Potential issue: In update, if `e_m > old_E`, set `rem[p] = res_c % pk`. But what if old_E > 0 and e_m > old_E, and `res_c % pk` not congruent to old rem? We argued no. Good.

Potential issue: If a cycle length m has prime p with e_m and old_E=0, e_g=0, so p not in g. But update sets rem[p]. Good.

Potential issue: If a cycle length m=1, fac empty, g=1, target=0, scan vals length1, res_c=0, update none. Good.

Potential issue: `cycle_vals` and `cycles` are lists of lists; `del A` okay.

Potential issue: If N=200000, recursion no.

Now, let's think about if the final code should include comments? Fine.

Let's maybe run through sample1 with code mentally:
N=6, spf. cycles as before. res. i0 cid0 m4 fac{2:2}, g1, congruences empty, r_mod_g0, target0, vals [4,1,2,3], best_t1, res_c1, update max_exp[2]=2 rem[2]=1.
i1 cid0 res set skip.
i2 cid0 skip.
i3 cid1 m2 fac{2:1}, E=max_exp[2]=2, e_g=1, pk2, g2, congruences [(2, rem[2]%2=1)], CRT x=1, r_mod_g1, off_i0 target1, vals [6,5], best_t1, res_c1, update e_m1 <= old_E2 no. Output. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with `res` for cycles that are length1 and first occurrence after some cycles. m=1, fac empty, g=1, r_mod_g0, target0, vals length1, best_t0, res_c0, update none. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with `max_exp` and `rem` arrays if p is large and N small? p <= m <= N. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible hidden issue with the "lexicographically smallest" if A values are 1-index and output should be separated by spaces; yes.

Now, final answer code block.</think>```python
import sys
from math import isqrt

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    N = data[0]
    P = [data[i + 1] - 1 for i in range(N)]
    A = [data[1 + N + i] for i in range(N)]
    del data

    spf = list(range(N + 1))
    if N >= 1:
        spf[1] = 1
    for i in range(2, isqrt(N) + 1):
        if spf[i] == i:
            for j in range(i * i, N + 1, i):
                if spf[j] == j:
                    spf[j] = i

    def factorize(x):
        res = {}
        while x > 1:
            p = spf[x]
            c = 0
            while x % p == 0:
                x //= p
                c += 1
            res[p] = c
        return res

    visited = [False] * N
    cycles = []
    cycle_id = [-1] * N
    offset = [0] * N
    cycle_len = []

    for i in range(N):
        if not visited[i]:
            cur = i
            cyc = []
            while not visited[cur]:
                visited[cur] = True
                cycle_id[cur] = len(cycles)
                offset[cur] = len(cyc)
                cyc.append(cur)
                cur = P[cur]
            cycles.append(cyc)
            cycle_len.append(len(cyc))

    cycle_vals = []
    for cyc in cycles:
        cycle_vals.append([A[idx] for idx in cyc])

    del A, P, visited

    C = len(cycles)
    res = [-1] * C

    max_exp = [0] * (N + 1)
    rem = [0] * (N + 1)

    for i in range(N):
        cid = cycle_id[i]
        if res[cid] != -1:
            continue

        m = cycle_len[cid]
        fac = factorize(m)

        g = 1
        congruences = []
        for p, e_m in fac.items():
            E = max_exp[p]
            e_g = E if E < e_m else e_m
            if e_g:
                pk = p ** e_g
                g *= pk
                congruences.append((pk, rem[p] % pk))

        x = 0
        mod = 1
        for pk, a in congruences:
            if mod == 1:
                x = a % pk
                mod = pk
            else:
                t = ((a - x) % pk) * pow(mod % pk, -1, pk) % pk
                x += mod * t
                mod *= pk

        r_mod_g = x
        target = (offset[i] + r_mod_g) % g

        vals = cycle_vals[cid]
        best_t = -1
        best_val = N + 1
        for t, val in enumerate(vals):
            if t % g == target and val < best_val:
                best_val = val
                best_t = t

        res_c = (best_t - offset[i]) % m
        res[cid] = res_c

        for p, e_m in fac.items():
            old_E = max_exp[p]
            if e_m > old_E:
                pk = p ** e_m
                rem[p] = res_c % pk
                max_exp[p] = e_m

    ans = [0] * N
    for c, cyc in enumerate(cycles):
        m = cycle_len[c]
        r = res[c]
        vals = cycle_vals[c]
        for t, idx in enumerate(cyc):
            ans[idx] = vals[(t + r) % m]

    sys.stdout.write(" ".join(map(str, ans)) + "\n")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
